<a id="sec:introduction"></a>
### Introduction

In a mean-payoff game, two players choose edges of a finite graph and compete over the long-run average weight of the resulting infinite walk. The objective is numerical, but the question of who can enforce a nonnegative average is a finite decision problem. A central algorithmic issue is the representation of the weights: a procedure polynomial in their absolute values may take exponential time in the length of the input. We give a deterministic algorithm whose running time is quasipolynomial in that binary length and which determines all winning starting vertices together.

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
#### The problem and the main theorem

Let $G=(V,E)$ be a finite directed graph with $V=\{1,\ldots,n\}$ and $n\ge1$. Each vertex has at least one outgoing edge and belongs to exactly one of $V_{\mathrm{Max}}$ and $V_{\mathrm{Min}}$. The edge weights $w:E\to\mathbb Z$ are arbitrary signed binary integers. Self-loops and parallel edges are allowed; parallel edges are separate entries of the explicit edge list. At a vertex its owner chooses the next edge. Both players’ strategies may depend on the entire finite history. For an infinite play $\pi=(e_0,e_1,\ldots)$, write $$\mathop{\mathrm{MP}}_w(\pi)=\liminf_{T\to\infty}\frac1T
                         \sum_{t=0}^{T-1}w(e_t).$$ We count the complete input length $L$ in a fixed conventional binary encoding of the graph, endpoints, ownership, and weights. For a start $v$ and strategies $\sigma,\tau$, denote their induced play by $\pi_{v,\sigma,\tau}$.

<a id="stmt-thm-main"></a>
<div id="thm:main" class="theorem">

**Theorem 1**. *A uniform deterministic algorithm returns exactly the set $$U=\bigl\{v\in V:\exists\text{ Max strategy }\sigma\ 
       \forall\text{ Min strategies }\tau,
       \ \mathop{\mathrm{MP}}_w(\pi_{v,\sigma,\tau})\ge0\bigr\}$$ for every game specified above. For an absolute constant $C>0$, the complete computation uses at most $$2^{C(\log_2(L+2))^2}$$ bit operations in a standard model polynomially equivalent to a uniform deterministic Turing machine. The bound includes preprocessing, exact arithmetic, recursion, and output of the entire set $U$.*

</div>

A positional strategy chooses its outgoing edge using only the current vertex. Section <a href="#sec:full-solution" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:full-solution">7</a> derives the exact rational value at every starting vertex and globally optimal positional strategies for both players, within the same quasipolynomial bit bound. Section <a href="#sec:energy-credits" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:energy-credits">8</a> decides whether a prescribed initial credit can keep every finite accumulated weight nonnegative in a one-resource energy game, and computes minimum winning credits. Section <a href="#sec:tropical" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:tropical">9</a> applies classical reductions to tropical feasibility and fixed-corank tropical-rank tests. A polynomial bound for the complete binary input would be a further improvement.

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
#### Earlier algorithms and binary weights

Ehrenfeucht and Mycielski proved the existence of positional optimal strategies for mean-payoff games (Ehrenfeucht and Mycielski 1979) <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[EhrenfeuchtMycielski1979](#ref-EhrenfeuchtMycielski1979)]. Positional determinacy places the threshold problem in $\mathrm{NP}\cap\mathrm{coNP}$. Zwick and Paterson developed pseudopolynomial algorithms and complexity reductions (Zwick and Paterson 1996) <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[ZwickPaterson1996](#ref-ZwickPaterson1996)]. Such bounds can be polynomial in the number of vertices and the largest absolute weight while remaining exponential in the binary encoding length. This distinction is central to Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.

The energy formulation asks whether a finite initial credit can keep all finite accumulated weights nonnegative. Brim, Chaloupka, Doyen, Gentilini, and Raskin developed bounded integer progress measures and monotone lifting, with an $O(|E|nW_0)$ threshold and winning-region bound when $W_0$ bounds the absolute weights (Brim et al. 2011, Theorem 8) <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[BrimEtAl2011](#ref-BrimEtAl2011)]. Dorfman, Kaplan, and Zwick developed a deterministic exponential-time approach based on accelerated potential updates and scaling; Austin and Dell’Erba corrected its update procedure (Dorfman, Kaplan, and Zwick 2019; Austin and Dell’Erba 2023) <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[DorfmanKaplanZwick2019](#ref-DorfmanKaplanZwick2019), [AustinDellErba2023](#ref-AustinDellErba2023)]. Kozachinskiy gives a polyhedral interpretation and a weight-independent $n^{O(1)}2^{n/2}$ arithmetic bound for energy winners (Kozachinskiy 2021) <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[Kozachinskiy2021](#ref-Kozachinskiy2021)].

Strategy-improvement algorithms provide another important comparison. Björklund and Vorobyov proved an expected $2^{O(\sqrt{n\log n})}$ arithmetic bound for the threshold partition on graphs without parallel edges (Björklund and Vorobyov 2007, Theorem 7.1) <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[BjorklundVorobyov2007](#ref-BjorklundVorobyov2007)], following work with Sandberg (Björklund, Sandberg, and Vorobyov 2004) <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[BjorklundSandbergVorobyov2004](#ref-BjorklundSandbergVorobyov2004)]. Recent analyses concern Random-Action-Removal (Zwick 2026) <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[Zwick2026](#ref-Zwick2026)] and Switch-All and Random-Edge for energy games (Dorfman, Kaplan, and Zwick 2026) <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[DorfmanKaplanZwick2026](#ref-DorfmanKaplanZwick2026)]. Iteration counts and arithmetic bounds must be distinguished from bit bounds: strategy evaluation and the sizes of intermediate numbers contribute to the latter.

Parity games use an extremal priority occurring infinitely often to determine the winner. They reduce in polynomial time to mean-payoff threshold games using weights of exponential magnitude but polynomial binary length (Jurdziński 1998, sec. 3) <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[Jurdzinski1998](#ref-Jurdzinski1998)]. Calude, Jain, Khoussainov, Li, and Stephan gave a quasipolynomial algorithm for parity games (Calude et al. 2022) <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[CaludeJainKhoussainovLiStephan2022](#ref-CaludeJainKhoussainovLiStephan2022)]. The reduction goes from parity to mean payoff, so this result does not by itself give a quasipolynomial algorithm for arbitrary binary-weight mean-payoff games. Daviaud, Jurdziński, and Lazić combined succinct parity progress measures with energy bounds in a pseudo-quasipolynomial algorithm for mean-payoff parity games (Daviaud, Jurdziński, and Lazić 2018) <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[DaviaudJurdzinskiLazic2018](#ref-DaviaudJurdzinskiLazic2018)]; that bound still contains a numerical weight parameter. Smoothed polynomial results for independent Gaussian perturbations of payoffs on ergodic game graphs concern a different input model (Loff and Skomra 2024) <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[LoffSkomra2024](#ref-LoffSkomra2024)].

<a id="pm-h-5"></a>
#### How the proof works

An integer potential assigns a number to each vertex. First replace $w(e)$ by $\widehat w(e)=(n+1)w(e)+1$, which makes every simple cycle sum nonzero while separating negative original sums from nonnegative ones. For $z\in\mathbb Z^V$, let $F_i(z)$ be the maximum of $\widehat w(e)+z_j$ over edges $e:i\to j$ at a Max vertex, and the minimum at a Min vertex. For integer vectors $A\le u$, the box $[A,u]$ consists of all integer vectors between them, coordinatewise. In this box, a subsolution is a vector $x\in[A,u]$ satisfying $x_i\le F_i(x)$ wherever $x_i>A_i$; a supersolution is a vector $y\in[A,u]$ satisfying $y_i\ge F_i(y)$ wherever $y_i<u_i$. Clipping a vector to the box means replacing coordinate $z_i$ by $\min\{u_i,\max\{A_i,z_i\}\}$. The comparison theorem in *Randomized quasipolynomial-time mean-payoff games*, our companion article, gives a unique fixed point $h$ of $z\mapsto\min\{u,\max\{A,F(z)\}\}$, and the inequalities $x\le h\le y$ for every such pair. These finite-box facts and their basic iteration procedure are the only results imported from the companion in the proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> (OpenAI 2026, Definition 2.2 and Lemmas 2.3–2.4). Section <a href="#sec:boxes" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:boxes">2</a> states that interface in full.

The same section places each coordinate of $h$ near one of the two boundaries of a sufficiently wide box $[0,D]^V$. The upper band is winning for Max and the lower band for Min. Iterating the clipped operator from the bottom can require a number of steps proportional to the numerical width $D$. We instead ask which boundary labels are forced, without computing $h$.

For a box $[A,A+D]$, give the vertices positive rational masses $a_i$ and $b_i$. For a vertex set $S$, its masses are $a(S)=\sum_{i\in S}a_i$ and $b(S)=\sum_{i\in S}b_i$. A Max witness is a subsolution whose support $\{i:x_i>A_i\}$ has $a$-mass at most one; it requires a positive label at coordinates near the upper boundary. A Min witness is the dual supersolution with support $\{i:y_i<A_i+D\}$ of $b$-mass at most one; it requires a negative label near the lower boundary. The task is to meet every requirement of every witness at once. With initial masses $1/n$, the box solution qualifies on both sides, so these requirements determine every vertex’s winner.

To solve that task recursively, we move an integer pivot vector $k$ near the middle of the box. For a Max witness, a common translation aligns the largest deviation $x_i-k_i$ with the top of a narrower box centered at $k$. Clipping at the bottom leaves only coordinates whose deviations are close to that largest value. A dual operation handles Min witnesses. Section <a href="#sec:translations" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:translations">3</a> proves that these operations preserve the required inequalities and restrict supports to subsets of the original supports.

The algorithm in Section <a href="#sec:algorithm" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:algorithm">4</a> first moves $k$ downward, then upward. Narrow recursive calls choose which coordinates move. Each pass protects the extremal deviation of all witnesses of one type. When a pass stops, any still-needed witness of the other type must have a set of coordinates close to an extremum with $a$-mass greater than $3/4$ for a Max witness or $b$-mass greater than $3/4$ for a Min witness. Otherwise the last recursive call would have forced another movement. This use of the final, stationary call is the key deterministic certificate.

One call on a half-width box labels those large-mass sets. Its labels need not settle the original boundary requirements. They instead specify new masses: coordinates labelled in a witness’s favor become lighter, and the others heavier. Section <a href="#sec:correctness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:correctness">5</a> shows that every unsettled witness still has support mass at most one. Meanwhile, every product $a_i b_i$ increases by a fixed factor. Once every product exceeds one, each coordinate is excluded from at least one type of witness support, and the labels can be chosen directly. Starting with masses $1/n$, only $O(\log(n+1))$ such product increases can therefore occur along a branch. The final call on the original box uses the reweighted masses to settle the remaining requirements. Every child except the half-width call increases the products; that exceptional child instead reduces the width. Section <a href="#sec:complexity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:complexity">6</a> counts the possible positions of the product increases along a branch and bounds the exact arithmetic in each call, giving the stated bit complexity.

<a id="pm-h-6"></a>
#### Potential methods and recursive precision

Gurvich, Karzanov, and Khachiyan developed potential transformations in their algorithm for cyclic games (Gurvich, Karzanov, and Khachiyan 1988; Karzanov and Lebedev 1993) <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[GurvichKarzanovKhachiyan1988](#ref-GurvichKarzanovKhachiyan1988), [KarzanovLebedev1993](#ref-KarzanovLebedev1993)]: adding differences of endpoint potentials to edge weights preserves cycle sums while changing the local inequalities. Paired potentials appear in the work of Lifshits and Pavlov (Lifshits and Pavlov 2007) <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[LifshitsPavlov2007](#ref-LifshitsPavlov2007)]. Cadilhac, Casares, and Ohlmann give a general framework for accelerated value iteration (Cadilhac, Casares, and Ohlmann 2025) <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[CadilhacCasaresOhlmann2025](#ref-CadilhacCasaresOhlmann2025)]. These methods and the progress-measure approach described above provide the setting for our finite-box inequalities.

The closest recursive ancestry is Parys’s reduced-precision parity algorithm (Parys 2019, Algorithm 2, Section 5, and Lemma 6.1) <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[Parys2019](#ref-Parys2019)] and its development by Lehtinen, Parys, Schewe, and Wojtczak (Lehtinen et al. 2022) <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[LehtinenParysScheweWojtczak2022](#ref-LehtinenParysScheweWojtczak2022)]. Their guarantees protect all dominions—sets within which one player can keep play and win— within specified size bounds, and their recursion separates one child retaining full precision from children of reduced precision. We likewise use simultaneous obligations for small objects, but smallness here means rational mass of a potential’s support. Translation near an extremal deviation, the stationary-pivot certificates, and reweighting establish the needed progress for that notion of smallness.

Ohlmann’s symmetric mean-payoff recursion supplies closely related potential-based context (Ohlmann 2026) <a id="pm-cite-19"></a>[[Ohlmann2026](#ref-Ohlmann2026)]; its subexponential time analysis is left open in the cited version. Jurdziński, Morvan, Ohlmann, and Thejaswini use two simultaneous labellings for symmetric acceleration of parity algorithms (Jurdziński et al. 2020) <a id="pm-cite-20"></a>[[JurdzinskiMorvanOhlmannThejaswini2020](#ref-JurdzinskiMorvanOhlmannThejaswini2020)]; a related development by Thejaswini, Ohlmann, and Jurdziński retains both decompositions to accelerate symmetric recursion (Thejaswini, Ohlmann, and Jurdziński 2022) <a id="pm-cite-21"></a>[[ThejaswiniOhlmannJurdzinski2022](#ref-ThejaswiniOhlmannJurdzinski2022)]. Arnold, Niwiński, and Parys study recursive interval restriction in a quasipolynomial black-box algorithm for nested fixed-point evaluation (Arnold, Niwiński, and Parys 2021) <a id="pm-cite-22"></a>[[ArnoldNiwinskiParys2021](#ref-ArnoldNiwinskiParys2021)]. Their bound is expressed in Boolean-lattice dimension and fixed-point nesting depth. The integer box here has chains of length $nD+1$, so that result does not directly give the dependence on $\log D$ established by our translation and mass arguments.

Fijalkow, Gawrychowski, and Ohlmann studied mean-payoff value iteration using universal graphs and lower bounds for those representations (Fijalkow, Gawrychowski, and Ohlmann 2020) <a id="pm-cite-23"></a>[[FijalkowGawrychowskiOhlmann2020](#ref-FijalkowGawrychowskiOhlmann2020)]. Those lower bounds constrain universal-graph representations. The present recursion constructs and reweights boxes for the given instance without using such a representation.

The companion algorithm (OpenAI 2026) <a id="pm-cite-24"></a>[[OpenAI2026Randomized](#ref-OpenAI2026Randomized)] returns potential vectors with probabilistic comparison guarantees stated separately for each fixed admissible vector. Here the recursive guarantee is simultaneous over all qualifying witnesses and concerns their boundary labels. The two algorithms share the finite-box theory stated in Section <a href="#sec:boxes" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:boxes">2</a>; the companion’s randomized proof is independent of the deterministic recursion. Section <a href="#sec:full-solution" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:full-solution">7</a> also explains how its winning-set interface yields randomized exact values and positional strategies.

<a id="sec:boxes"></a>
### From potential comparisons to a labelling problem

This section specifies the finite-box input used from the companion, then proves the game reduction at the width required by our recursion. The outcome is a labelling problem whose obligations are expressed by potential vectors. Later sections solve that problem without finding the potential that certifies the winning partition.

<a id="pm-h-8"></a>
#### The common finite-box interface

<a id="eq:cycle-signs"></a>
Fix the input graph and ownership throughout. Following the integer cycle perturbation of Björklund and Vorobyov (Björklund and Vorobyov 2007, sec. 9) <a id="pm-cite-25"></a>[[BjorklundVorobyov2007](#ref-BjorklundVorobyov2007)], put <a id="eq:bias"></a>$$\widehat w(e)=(n+1)w(e)+1,
 \qquad W=\max_{e\in E}|\widehat w(e)|.
 \label{eq:bias}$$ The edge set is nonempty. Each transformed weight is a nonzero integer, so $W\ge1$. If a simple directed cycle has length $\ell$ and original sum $c$, then $$\widehat w(C)=(n+1)c+\ell,\qquad 1\le\ell\le n.
 \label{eq:cycle-signs}$$ For $c\ge0$ the transformed sum is positive; for $c\le-1$ it is negative. Thus no simple cycle has zero transformed sum, and positive transformed sums correspond exactly to nonnegative original sums. A self-loop has $\ell=1$, and the calculation applies to every actual edge sequence when parallel edges are present.

<a id="eq:operator"></a>
<a id="eq:operator-properties"></a>
For all $z\in\mathbb Z^V$, define $$F_i(z)=
 \begin{cases}
  \displaystyle\max_{e:i\to j}(\widehat w(e)+z_j),&i\in V_{\mathrm{Max}},\\[2mm]
  \displaystyle\min_{e:i\to j}(\widehat w(e)+z_j),&i\in V_{\mathrm{Min}}.
 \end{cases}
 \label{eq:operator}$$ The finite, nonempty outgoing-edge sets ensure that these extrema are attained. With coordinatewise vector order and common scalar shifts, $$z\le z'\ \Longrightarrow\ F(z)\le F(z'),
 \qquad F(z+t)=F(z)+t\quad(t\in\mathbb Z).
 \label{eq:operator-properties}$$ For integer vectors $A\le u$, the notation $[A,u]$ denotes all integer vectors between them. A *subsolution* is a full vector $x\in[A,u]$ satisfying $x_i\le F_i(x)$ at every coordinate where $x_i>A_i$. A *supersolution* is a full vector $y\in[A,u]$ satisfying $y_i\ge F_i(y)$ wherever $y_i<u_i$. No inequality is required on the respective excluded boundary. These conventions agree with the companion’s Definition 2.2 (OpenAI 2026) <a id="pm-cite-26"></a>[[OpenAI2026Randomized](#ref-OpenAI2026Randomized)].

<a id="stmt-lem-accepted-box"></a>
<div id="lem:accepted-box" class="lemma">

**Lemma 2** (Finite-box comparison and basic iteration). *For the operator in Equation <a href="#eq:operator" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:operator">[eq:operator]</a> and arbitrary finite integer bounds $A\le u$, every subsolution $x$ and supersolution $y$ in $[A,u]$ satisfy $x_i\le y_i$ for all $i\in V$. There is a unique $h\in[A,u]$ that is both a subsolution and a supersolution. It is precisely the unique fixed point of $$T_{A,u}(z)=\min\{u,\max\{A,F(z)\}\}.$$ Consequently every such pair satisfies $x\le h\le y$.*

*The following synchronous iteration finds $h$. Start with $p=A$; evaluate $q=T_{A,u}(p)$; return $p$ if $q=p$, and otherwise set $p=q$ and repeat. It uses at most $$1+\sum_{i\in V}(u_i-A_i)$$ evaluations of $T_{A,u}$, including the final stability test.*

</div>

We use this statement from the complete September 25 companion, Lemmas 2.3–2.4 (OpenAI 2026) <a id="pm-cite-27"></a>[[OpenAI2026Randomized](#ref-OpenAI2026Randomized)]. It permits negative endpoints and collapsed coordinates $A_i=u_i$. In particular its comparison conclusion concerns all vertices, even though each hypothesis is imposed only on its stated support. The operator and integer hypotheses are exactly those above, and Equation <a href="#eq:cycle-signs" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cycle-signs">[eq:cycle-signs]</a> verifies the required absence of zero simple-cycle sums. The sandwich inequality follows by comparing with $h$ on each side. We call $h$ the *box solution* and the iteration $\ensuremath{\mathsf{Basic}}$. The algorithm will invoke $\ensuremath{\mathsf{Basic}}$ only when the box width is bounded by an absolute constant.

<a id="pm-h-9"></a>
#### The two bands of a wide box

<a id="eq:initial-width"></a>
Choose the initial dyadic width $$D_0=\min\{2^d:d\in\mathbb Z_{\ge0},\ 2^d\ge64(n+1)(W+1)\}.
 \label{eq:initial-width}$$ The next proposition establishes the game reduction for this width. The companion uses width $8nW$ in its own reduction; that different choice is not an input to the proof here.

<a id="stmt-prop-game-reduction"></a>
<div id="prop:game-reduction" class="proposition">

**Proposition 3** (Winning partition from the wide box). *Let $h$ be the box solution in $[0,D_0]^V$. Then $$V_{\mathrm{lo}}=\{i:h_i\le nW\},\qquad
 V_{\mathrm{hi}}=\{i:h_i\ge D_0-nW\}$$ partition $V$. There is a positional Max strategy under which every play starting in $V_{\mathrm{hi}}$ has original mean payoff at least zero. There is a positional Min strategy under which every play starting in $V_{\mathrm{lo}}$ has original upper limiting average at most $-1/n$. Both assertions allow arbitrary history-dependent opposition.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-game-reduction"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-game-reduction</summary>

*Proof.* We first locate the coordinates of $h$, then construct the two strategies. If $0<h_i<D_0$, both box inequalities apply and give $h_i=F_i(h)$. Follow an edge attaining that equality and continue from its head as long as the current coordinate is interior. A first repeated vertex would yield a simple cycle of tight equalities $h_i=\widehat w(e)+h_j$, with transformed sum zero. This is impossible by Equation <a href="#eq:cycle-signs" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cycle-signs">[eq:cycle-signs]</a>. A boundary is therefore reached within $n$ edges. Each edge changes the potential by at most $W$. Including the length-zero path from a boundary coordinate, every coordinate is within $nW$ of either $0$ or $D_0$. Since $D_0>2nW$, the two bands are disjoint and exhaust $V$.

<a id="eq:high-edge"></a>
Every high coordinate is above the lower boundary. At a high Max vertex choose one outgoing edge satisfying $$h_i\le\widehat w(e)+h_j;
 \label{eq:high-edge}$$ such an edge exists by the subsolution inequality. At a high Min vertex every outgoing edge satisfies this inequality. Each of these permitted edges has $$h_j\ge D_0-(n+1)W>nW,$$ so its head is high by the partition just proved. Fixing the Max choices and extending them arbitrarily outside the high band gives a positional strategy keeping all consistent plays from high vertices in that band.

On a simple cycle of permitted edges, Equation <a href="#eq:high-edge" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:high-edge">[eq:high-edge]</a> telescopes to a nonnegative transformed sum. Its original sum is therefore nonnegative. Let $W_0=\max_e|w(e)|$. Delete simple cycles successively from any finite play prefix until fewer than $n$ edges remain. The removed cycles have nonnegative original sums, and the remaining walk has weight at least $-(n-1)W_0$. Hence every prefix of every consistent play has at least that weight. Dividing by its length and taking a lower limit proves Max’s assertion.

For the other band, every low coordinate is below the upper boundary. At a low Min vertex fix an edge attaining the minimum in $F_i(h)$, and at a low Max vertex permit all outgoing edges. The supersolution inequality gives <a id="eq:low-edge"></a>$$h_i\ge\widehat w(e)+h_j
 \label{eq:low-edge}$$ for each permitted edge. Its head satisfies $h_j\le(n+1)W<D_0-nW$, so it is low. These choices, extended arbitrarily elsewhere, give a positional Min strategy preserving the low band.

Every simple cycle of permitted edges now has nonpositive transformed sum and hence original integer sum at most $-1$. Deleting simple cycles from a prefix of length $T$ leaves at most $n-1$ edges. Each deleted cycle has at most $n$ edges, so the number removed is at least $(T-(n-1))/n$. The original prefix weight is at most $$-\frac{T-(n-1)}n+(n-1)W_0.$$ Its upper limiting average is at most $-1/n$. The prefix estimates on both sides apply to every consistent edge sequence, independently of how the opposing player uses the history. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-10"></a>
#### Simultaneous boundary obligations

<a id="eq:recursive-input"></a>
The recursion works on inputs $$A\in\mathbb Z^V,\qquad D=2^d\ (d\in\mathbb Z_{\ge0}),\qquad
 a,b\in\mathbb Q_{>0}^V.
 \label{eq:recursive-input}$$ Its box is $[A,u]$ with $u=A+D$, and its claim margin is $P=D/32$. For $S\subseteq V$ let $a(S)=\sum_{i\in S}a_i$ and $b(S)=\sum_{i\in S}b_i$. The positive rational vectors $a,b$ are called *masses*.

<a id="stmt-def-witnesses"></a>
<div id="def:witnesses" class="definition">

**Definition 4** (Witnesses and their claims). A *Max witness* for $(A,D,a,b)$ is a subsolution $x\in[A,u]$ with support $S_x=\{i:x_i>A_i\}$ and $a(S_x)\le1$. It *claims* each $i$ with $x_i\ge u_i-P$. A *Min witness* is a supersolution $y\in[A,u]$ with support $S_y=\{i:y_i<u_i\}$ and $b(S_y)\le1$. It claims each $i$ with $y_i\le A_i+P$. A witness is *relevant* when it claims at least one index.

</div>

Since $P<D$, every claim belongs to its witness’s support. Claims of opposite types cannot coincide: otherwise box comparison would give $u_i-P\le x_i\le y_i\le A_i+P$, contradicting $2P<D$. Masses limit the witnesses covered by an obligation; the graph itself always retains every vertex and edge.

<a id="stmt-thm-labelling"></a>
<div id="thm:labelling" class="theorem">

**Theorem 5** (Simultaneous labelling). *For every input in Equation <a href="#eq:recursive-input" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:recursive-input">[eq:recursive-input]</a>, the procedure $\ensuremath{\mathsf{Label}}(A,D,a,b)$ of Section <a href="#sec:algorithm" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:algorithm">4</a> terminates and returns a vector in $\{+,-\}^V$. It assigns $+$ to every index claimed by any Max witness and $-$ to every index claimed by any Min witness.*

</div>

All witnesses of a call are covered by this one output. They are neither enumerated nor supplied to the procedure. On vertices with no claim the theorem permits either label.

<a id="eq:main-call"></a>
Apply this guarantee to $$A=0,\qquad D=D_0,\qquad a_i=b_i=1/n.
 \label{eq:main-call}$$ Every support then has mass at most one. The box solution $h$ is therefore a witness of both types. Moreover $P=D_0/32\ge nW$, so its Max claims include all high vertices and its Min claims include all low vertices. By Proposition <a href="#prop:game-reduction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:game-reduction">3</a>, a labelling satisfying Theorem <a href="#thm:labelling" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:labelling">5</a> returns the exact winning set by taking its positively labelled vertices. This uses the existence and boundary location of $h$, without executing $\ensuremath{\mathsf{Basic}}$ on the wide initial box.

<a id="sec:translations"></a>
### Moving witnesses to a narrower box

A recursive child will receive a box centered at a pivot $k$ near the middle of its parent box. To transfer a parent’s obligations to this child, we translate a witness by one scalar and clip it at a boundary. This section establishes the exact support and claims of the translated vector. It is the geometric part of the argument and uses only monotonicity, common-shift invariance, and the witness definition.

<a id="eq:pivot-parameters"></a>
Consider an input $(A,D,a,b)$ with dyadic $D\ge4096$. In addition to $u=A+D$ and $P=D/32$, fix $$c=A+D/2,\qquad M=D/8,\qquad q=D/128,\qquad
 p=q/32=D/4096.
 \label{eq:pivot-parameters}$$ The vector $c$ is the center, $M$ bounds the pivot’s displacement, $q$ is the narrow child width, and $p$ will be one pivot step. All these quantities, as well as $q/2$ and $D/4$, are integral. We consider integer pivots $c-M\le k\le c+M$.

<a id="eq:max-gaps"></a>
<a id="eq:min-gaps"></a>
For a relevant Max witness $x$, let $$s_x(k)=\max_{i\in V}(x_i-k_i),\qquad
 g_i^x(k)=s_x(k)-(x_i-k_i).
 \label{eq:max-gaps}$$ We call $s_x(k)$ its *peak* and $g_i^x(k)$ its gap at $i$. For a relevant Min witness $y$ the dual definitions are $$s_y(k)=\max_{i\in V}(k_i-y_i),\qquad
 g_i^y(k)=s_y(k)-(k_i-y_i).
 \label{eq:min-gaps}$$ Every gap is a nonnegative integer. The following lemma treats the two widths that the recursion will use.

<a id="stmt-lem-translation"></a>
<div id="lem:translation" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:max-translation"></a>
**Lemma 6** (Translated supports and claims). *Let $(A,D,a,b)$ be an input with $D\ge4096$, let $c-M\le k\le c+M$ be integral, and let $r\in\{q,D/2\}$. For any relevant Max witness $x$, the vector $$x_i'=\max\{k_i-r/2,\ x_i-s_x(k)+r/2\}
 \label{eq:max-translation}$$ is an integer subsolution in $[k-r/2,k+r/2]$. Its support is $$S_{x'}=\{i:g_i^x(k)<r\}\subseteq S_x.$$ For any positive new Max masses $a'$ with $a'(S_{x'})\le1$, it is a Max witness and claims every $i$ with $g_i^x(k)\le r/32$.*

*For any relevant Min witness $y$, the vector <a id="eq:min-translation"></a>$$y_i'=\min\{k_i+r/2,\ y_i+s_y(k)-r/2\}
 \label{eq:min-translation}$$ is an integer supersolution in the same box, with support $$S_{y'}=\{i:g_i^y(k)<r\}\subseteq S_y.$$ For any positive new Min masses $b'$ with $b'(S_{y'})\le1$, it is a Min witness and claims every $i$ with $g_i^y(k)\le r/32$. In particular, the corresponding unchanged masses $a$ and $b$ always make these translated vectors witnesses.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-translation"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-translation</summary>

<a id="eq:outside-gap"></a>
*Proof.* The support assertion requires knowing which coordinates could survive the clipping. Relevance supplies that information. A Max claim at $j$ gives $x_j\ge A_j+D-P$, while $k_j\le A_j+D/2+M$; hence <a id="eq:peak-lower"></a>$$s_x(k)\ge D/2-M-P.
 \label{eq:peak-lower}$$ If $i\notin S_x$, then $x_i=A_i$ and $x_i-k_i\le-D/2+M$. Combining the two inequalities gives $$g_i^x(k)\ge D-2M-P=23D/32>D/2.
 \label{eq:outside-gap}$$ The untruncated term in Equation <a href="#eq:max-translation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:max-translation">[eq:max-translation]</a> is $$k_i+r/2-g_i^x(k).$$ It lies below or at the new upper boundary. Taking its maximum with the lower boundary puts $x'$ inside the new box, with support exactly where $g_i^x(k)<r$. Since $r\le D/2$, Equation <a href="#eq:outside-gap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:outside-gap">[eq:outside-gap]</a> shows that this support lies in $S_x$. Integrality follows from the integral half-widths and peaks.

Write $t=-s_x(k)+r/2$. Globally $x'\ge x+t$; on its new support, $x_i'=x_i+t$ and the parent’s subsolution inequality applies. Equation <a href="#eq:operator-properties" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:operator-properties">[eq:operator-properties]</a> therefore yields $$F_i(x')\ge F_i(x+t)=F_i(x)+t
                                \ge x_i+t=x_i'.$$ Thus $x'$ is a subsolution. Its witness condition is precisely the stated mass bound. If $g_i^x(k)\le r/32$, its untruncated entry is at least $k_i+r/2-r/32$, the new Max claim threshold; taking a maximum with the lower boundary cannot destroy that claim.

For Min, relevance likewise gives $s_y(k)\ge D/2-M-P$. Outside $S_y$, one has $y_i=A_i+D$, so $k_i-y_i\le-D/2+M$. The gap is again at least $23D/32>D/2$. Now the translated term before clipping is $$k_i-r/2+g_i^y(k).$$ It is above or at the lower boundary, and taking its minimum with the upper boundary leaves support exactly at gaps $<r$. The support is contained in $S_y$ by the same outside-support estimate. Set $t=s_y(k)-r/2$. The vector inequality $y'\le y+t$ and equality $y_i'=y_i+t$ on the new support imply $$F_i(y')\le F_i(y+t)=F_i(y)+t
                                \le y_i+t=y_i'.$$ This is the required supersolution inequality. The mass condition makes $y'$ a witness, and a gap at most $r/32$ puts it at most $r/32$ above the new lower boundary, giving the asserted claim. Finally, support containment and positivity imply $a(S_{x'})\le a(S_x)\le1$ and $b(S_{y'})\le b(S_y)\le1$ for unchanged masses. ◻

</details>

The two thresholds serve different purposes: gaps strictly below $r$ describe the surviving support, whereas gaps at most $r/32$ give claims.

<a id="sec:algorithm"></a>
### A deterministic recursion on width and mass

We now give the procedure whose output meets the simultaneous obligations of Theorem <a href="#thm:labelling" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:labelling">5</a>. Every step uses exact integers or rationals and label vectors from children. The witnesses from the preceding section are proof objects; no execution searches for or tests them.

For an integer pivot $k$ and an even width $r$, a *centered call* with masses $(a',b')$ means $\ensuremath{\mathsf{Label}}(k-r/2,r,a',b')$, on the box $[k-r/2,k+r/2]$. This notation will only be used with integer half-widths.

In each pivot pass, one side’s masses remain unchanged. Its translated witnesses force labels at all gaps at most $p=q/32$; those labels will keep these coordinates fixed while the pivot moves. The later width-$D/2$ call has unchanged masses on both sides and makes all gaps below $q$ into claims, supplying the labels for reweighting.

<a id="stmt-step-continuation"></a>
<div id="step:continuation" class="procedure">

**Procedure 7** ($\ensuremath{\mathsf{Label}}(A,D,a,b)$). <span id="alg:label" label="alg:label"></span> Set $u=A+D$ and $P=D/32$. Execute the first applicable base case; if neither applies, proceed through Steps 3–6.

1.  <span id="step:mass-base" label="step:mass-base"></span>**Mass base case.** If $a_i b_i>1$ for all $i\in V$, return $+$ where $b_i>1$ and return $-$ at the remaining vertices.

2.  <span id="step:width-base" label="step:width-base"></span>**Width base case.** If $D<4096$, compute the box solution $z$ by $\ensuremath{\mathsf{Basic}}$. Return $+$ at $i$ if $z_i>A_i+D/2$, and $-$ otherwise.

3.  <span id="step:down" label="step:down"></span>**Downward pass.** Use $c,M,q,p$ from Equation <a href="#eq:pivot-parameters" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:pivot-parameters">[eq:pivot-parameters]</a> and initialize $k=c$. Repeatedly make a centered width-$q$ call with masses $(a,\frac43 b)$ and, from its labels, set simultaneously $$k_i^{\mathrm{new}}=
     \begin{cases}
       \max\{c_i-M,k_i-p\},&\text{if the label at }i\text{ is }-,\\
       k_i,&\text{if the label at }i\text{ is }+.
     \end{cases}$$ Replace $k$ by $k^{\mathrm{new}}$. Stop when the update leaves $k$ unchanged, denote this last pivot by $k^{\downarrow}$, and record $$I_- =\{i:k_i^{\downarrow}<c_i-P\}.$$

4.  <span id="step:up" label="step:up"></span>**Upward pass.** Start from $k=k^{\downarrow}$. Repeatedly make a centered width-$q$ call with masses $(\frac43 a,b)$ and set simultaneously $$k_i^{\mathrm{new}}=
     \begin{cases}
       \min\{c_i+M,k_i+p\},&\text{if the label at }i\text{ is }+,\\
       k_i,&\text{if the label at }i\text{ is }-.
     \end{cases}$$ Replace $k$ by $k^{\mathrm{new}}$. Stop when it is unchanged, write $k^f$ for the final pivot, and record $$I_+=\{i:k_i^f>c_i+P\}.$$

5.  <span id="step:provisional" label="step:provisional"></span>**Provisional labels and new masses.** Make one centered width-$D/2$ call at $k^f$ with masses $(a,b)$. For its returned vector $\lambda^0\in\{+,-\}^V$, define <a id="eq:reweighting"></a>$$(\bar a_i,\bar b_i)=
     \begin{cases}
      (\frac45 a_i,\frac85 b_i),&\lambda_i^0=+,\\[1mm]
      (\frac85 a_i,\frac45 b_i),&\lambda_i^0=-.
     \end{cases}
     \label{eq:reweighting}$$

6.  **Continuation and overrides.** Call $\ensuremath{\mathsf{Label}}(A,D,\bar a,\bar b)$. In that child’s output put $-$ on $I_-$ and $+$ on $I_+\setminus I_-$, leaving every other label unchanged. Return the resulting vector.

</div>

Each pass includes a final child call whose update changes no coordinate. Correctness will use that call’s guarantee together with the stationary update. The two passes run in the stated order; within one update every coordinate uses the same returned label vector and the same old pivot.

<a id="pm-h-13"></a>
#### Integral nested boxes and bounded passes

The next facts depend only on the mechanics of the procedure, not on the meaning of the returned labels.

<a id="stmt-lem-well-formed"></a>
<div id="lem:well-formed" class="lemma">

**Lemma 8**. *Every recursive child is a valid input of the form Equation <a href="#eq:recursive-input" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:recursive-input">[eq:recursive-input]</a>, and its box is contained in its parent’s box. In a nonbase call the pivot satisfies $$\begin{aligned}
 c-M\le k\le c&\qquad\text{during the downward pass},\\
 c-M\le k\le c+M&\qquad\text{during the upward pass}.
 \end{aligned}$$ If child calls terminate, each pass makes at most $1024n+1$ calls.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-well-formed"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-well-formed</summary>

*Proof.* In the recursive case, $D\ge4096$ is dyadic, so all parameters in Equation <a href="#eq:pivot-parameters" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:pivot-parameters">[eq:pivot-parameters]</a>, both child half-widths, and every pivot coordinate are integral. The child widths $D/128$ and $D/2$ are positive powers of two. The update caps imply the stated pivot bounds. A centered child has width $r\le D/2$, and its endpoints satisfy $$c-M-r/2\ge A+D/8\ge A,
 \qquad c+M+r/2\le A+7D/8\le A+D.$$ The continuation has the unchanged parent box. All mass multipliers preserve positivity and rationality.

The pivot lies on the grid of spacing $p$ through $c$, since $M/p=512$ is integral and both caps belong to that grid. In a changing update at least one coordinate moves by at least $p$. Each pass is coordinatewise monotone over a range of at most $2M$, so a coordinate moves at most $2M/p=1024$ times. At most $1024n$ updates can change the pivot. One further child call certifies the unchanged update at which the pass stops. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-14"></a>
#### A rank that decreases for every child

<a id="eq:budget"></a>
For positive masses define $$\mu(a,b)=\min_{i\in V}a_i b_i,
 \qquad \rho=\frac{32}{25},
 \qquad
 B(\mu)=
 \begin{cases}
  0,&\mu>1,\\
  1+\lfloor\log_{\rho}(1/\mu)\rfloor,&0<\mu\le1.
 \end{cases}
 \label{eq:budget}$$ The integer $B$ measures how many guaranteed increases of the minimum mass product can precede the mass base case. It is used for analysis only; the executable procedure does not evaluate logarithms.

<a id="stmt-lem-termination"></a>
<div id="lem:termination" class="lemma">

**Lemma 9** (Well-founded recursion). *The procedure terminates on every input in Equation <a href="#eq:recursive-input" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:recursive-input">[eq:recursive-input]</a>. Writing $D=2^d$, every child of a nonbase call has smaller rank $$R=d+B(\mu(a,b)).$$ The provisional child in Step 5 is the only child that preserves $B$. Every other child decreases $B$ by at least one.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-termination"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-termination</summary>

*Proof.* If $\mu>1$, the mass base case applies. Otherwise multiplying $\mu$ by a factor at least $\rho$ decreases $B$ by at least one: either the new value exceeds one, or its logarithm in Equation <a href="#eq:budget" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:budget">[eq:budget]</a> falls by at least one before rounding down.

In either pivot pass, each product $a_i b_i$ is multiplied by $4/3\ge\rho$. In the continuation, each product is multiplied by $(4/5)(8/5)=\rho$, independent of the provisional label. Thus these children decrease $B$ and do not increase $d$. The provisional child keeps the masses and halves the width, decreasing $d$ by one. Every child has strictly smaller nonnegative rank $R$.

Induction on $R$ now proves termination. Base cases require only finite arithmetic and, in the width case, the finite iteration of Lemma <a href="#lem:accepted-box" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:accepted-box">2</a>. At a nonbase node, every child terminates by induction. Lemma <a href="#lem:well-formed" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:well-formed">8</a> then bounds the two passes; the remaining two children and local operations are finite as well. This reasoning makes no correctness assumption about a label. ◻

</details>

The distinction between width reduction and budget reduction is shown in Figure <a href="#fig:recursion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:recursion">1</a>. It permits induction for correctness and a sharper count than a general branching bound: only one child can preserve the mass budget, irrespective of the width.

<figure id="fig:recursion">

<figcaption>Children of a nonbase call. The two passes together produce at most $2(1024n+1)$ narrow children, each decreasing $B$. The provisional child preserves $B$ and halves the width; the continuation preserves the box and decreases $B$. Edges show the recursion tree, not the execution order.</figcaption>
</figure>

<a id="pm-figsrc-fig-recursion"></a>
**Figure source and visual witness** (`fig:recursion`)

TikZ/PGF source, copied verbatim from the source:

```latex
\begin{tikzpicture}[
 >=Stealth,
 box/.style={draw=linkblue,rounded corners=2pt,align=center,
             font=\small,inner sep=6pt,text width=3.6cm},
 edge/.style={->,draw=linkblue,thick}]
 \node[box,text width=4.5cm] (root) at (0,0)
   {Parent input\\width $D$, mass budget $B$};
 \node[box] (loops) at (-4.4,-2.1)
   {Pivot-pass children\\$\le 2(1024n+1)$ calls\\
    width $D/128$\\budget $\le B-1$};
 \node[box] (middle) at (0,-2.1)
   {Provisional child\\One call\\width $D/2$\\budget $B$};
 \node[box] (last) at (4.4,-2.1)
   {Continuation\\One call\\width $D$\\budget $\le B-1$};
 \draw[edge] (root.south west) -- (loops.north);
 \draw[edge] (root.south) -- (middle.north);

\end{tikzpicture}
```

Visual witness description of PDF page 12 (generated from the PDF page, not source text):
