```text
A tree diagram depicting the recursion branching from a nonbase call. A top root box is labeled "Parent input / width $D$, mass budget $B$". Three arrows point downward from the root to three child boxes:
- Left child box: "Pivot-pass children / $\le 2(1024n + 1)$ calls / width $D/128$ / budget $\le B - 1$"
- Center child box: "Provisional child / One call / width $D/2$ / budget $B$"
- Right child box: "Continuation / One call / width $D$ / budget $\le B - 1$"
```


<a id="sec:correctness"></a>
### Why the labels satisfy every witness

Termination supplies a well-founded order for the correctness proof. At a nonbase call, assume that all children satisfy the simultaneous labelling guarantee. We will show first that the marks $I_-$ and $I_+$ can safely receive their prescribed labels. We will then show that every witness with a remaining claim survives the mass change. These two statements allow the continuation to finish the call.

In the next three lemmas, fix a nonbase input $(A,D,a,b)$ and assume Theorem <a href="#thm:labelling" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:labelling">5</a> for all its children. Witnesses, supports, and claims without a prime refer to this parent input. Peaks and gaps are those of Equations <a href="#eq:max-gaps" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:max-gaps">[eq:max-gaps]</a>–<a href="#eq:min-gaps" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:min-gaps">[eq:min-gaps]</a>.

<a id="pm-h-16"></a>
#### Protection during motion and mass at stopping

<a id="stmt-lem-down"></a>
<div id="lem:down" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:down-heavy"></a>
**Lemma 10** (The downward pass). *For every relevant Max witness $x$, its peak $s_x(k)$ is constant throughout Step 3 and at most $D/2$. In particular, no Max witness claims an index in $I_-$. For every Min witness $y$ that has a claim outside $I_-$, $$b\bigl(\{i:g_i^y(k^{\downarrow})<q\}\bigr)>3/4.
 \label{eq:down-heavy}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-down"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-down</summary>

*Proof.* Fix $x$ and consider one downward update from pivot $k$. Its width-$q$ translation is a witness for the child, since that child keeps Max masses $a$. By Lemma <a href="#lem:translation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:translation">6</a>, every coordinate of gap at most $q/32=p$ is a Max claim of the translated witness. The child’s guarantee labels these coordinates $+$, and they do not move. In particular at least one gap-zero coordinate continues to attain the old peak.

At every other coordinate, the downward movement $\delta_i$ lies in $[0,p]$. For its gap $g_i^x(k)>p$, $$x_i-k_i^{\mathrm{new}}
           =s_x(k)-g_i^x(k)+\delta_i<s_x(k).$$ Thus no coordinate can overtake the old peak, even though many may move in the same update. The peak is unchanged. Initially $k=c$ and $x\le A+D$, so $s_x(c)\le D/2$. If $x$ claims $i$, $$k_i^{\downarrow}\ge x_i-D/2
                    \ge A_i+D/2-P=c_i-P,$$ which excludes $i$ from $I_-$.

For the mass assertion, let $y$ claim $j\notin I_-$. The stopped pivot satisfies $k_j^{\downarrow}\ge c_j-P$, and the claim gives $y_j\le A_j+P$. Hence <a id="eq:down-uncapped"></a>$$s_y(k^{\downarrow})\ge D/2-2P>D/2-M.
 \label{eq:down-uncapped}$$ A coordinate at the lower cap would have $k_i^{\downarrow}-y_i\le D/2-M$, since $y_i\ge A_i$. Every maximizer of $k_i^{\downarrow}-y_i$ is therefore strictly above its cap.

Suppose the mass in Equation <a href="#eq:down-heavy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:down-heavy">[eq:down-heavy]</a> were at most $3/4$. The width-$q$ Min translation has exactly that support, so it would have support mass at most one for the last child’s masses $\frac43 b$. Its gap-zero maximizers would all have to receive $-$. At least one of those coordinates would then move down by a positive amount, because it is above the cap and $p>0$. This contradicts the final unchanged update. Equality at $3/4$ also leads to this contradiction, proving the strict mass bound. ◻

</details>

The downward marks are now safe, but the pivot will still move upward. The next lemma both protects the other type of claim and shows why the first mass certificate survives that later movement.

<a id="stmt-lem-up"></a>
<div id="lem:up" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:final-min-heavy"></a>
<a id="eq:final-max-heavy"></a>
**Lemma 11** (The upward pass). *For every relevant Min witness $y$, its peak $s_y(k)$ is constant throughout Step 4 and at most $D/2$. Thus no Min witness claims an index in $I_+$. At the final pivot, $$\begin{aligned}
 b\bigl(\{i:g_i^y(k^f)<q\}\bigr)&>3/4
   &&\text{if }y\text{ has a claim outside }I_-;
   \label{eq:final-min-heavy}\\
 a\bigl(\{i:g_i^x(k^f)<q\}\bigr)&>3/4
   &&\text{if }x\text{ has a claim outside }I_+.
   \label{eq:final-max-heavy}
\end{aligned}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-up"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-up</summary>

*Proof.* In every upward child the Min masses are unchanged. Translate a fixed relevant Min witness $y$ to that width-$q$ box. All gaps at most $p$ become Min claims, so those coordinates are labelled $-$ and stay fixed. Every other coordinate has gap greater than $p$ and moves upward by at most $p$. Its new deviation $k_i^{\mathrm{new}}-y_i$ is therefore strictly below the old peak. A gap-zero coordinate does not move, proving exact peak preservation. At the start, $k^{\downarrow}\le c$ and $y\ge A$ imply $s_y\le D/2$. A Min claim at $i$ consequently gives $$k_i^f\le y_i+D/2\le c_i+P,$$ so it is outside $I_+$.

Each coordinate $k_i$ is nondecreasing during this pass while $s_y$ is fixed. It follows that <a id="eq:min-gap-persistence"></a>$$g_i^y(k^f)\le g_i^y(k^{\downarrow}).
 \label{eq:min-gap-persistence}$$ The set of Min gaps $<q$ can only grow. Its $b$-mass was greater than $3/4$ at $k^{\downarrow}$ whenever $y$ had a claim outside $I_-$, by Lemma <a href="#lem:down" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:down">10</a>. Positivity of $b$ proves Equation <a href="#eq:final-min-heavy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:final-min-heavy">[eq:final-min-heavy]</a>.

It remains to obtain the Max mass certificate at this second stopping point. If $x$ claims $j\notin I_+$, then $x_j\ge A_j+D-P$ and $k_j^f\le c_j+P$, giving $$s_x(k^f)\ge D/2-2P>D/2-M.$$ At an upper-capped coordinate, the inequalities $k_i^f=c_i+M$ and $x_i\le A_i+D$ would give a deviation at most $D/2-M$. Thus every maximizer lies strictly below the upper cap. If the $a$-mass of the gaps $<q$ were at most $3/4$, the width-$q$ translation would be a Max witness for the final child’s masses $\frac43 a$. Its maximizers would receive $+$ and force a positive upward movement, contradicting the unchanged update. This proves Equation <a href="#eq:final-max-heavy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:final-max-heavy">[eq:final-max-heavy]</a>. ◻

</details>

The mass bounds are absolute: each qualifying set has mass greater than $3/4$, while the entire witness support has mass at most one. They are not merely fractional lower bounds relative to a support that might have much smaller mass. This strength is exactly what the reweighting calculation needs.

<a id="pm-h-17"></a>
#### Preserving the unsettled witnesses

The provisional child does not directly label the parent’s claims. Its purpose is to assign the favorable label on each large-mass set identified above. The larger width $D/2$ makes these sets claims of the translated witnesses.

<a id="stmt-lem-reweight"></a>
<div id="lem:reweight" class="lemma">

**Lemma 12** (Witness survival). *Every parent Max witness with a claim outside $I_+$ remains a Max witness for $(A,D,\bar a,\bar b)$. Every parent Min witness with a claim outside $I_-$ remains a Min witness for that input.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-reweight"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-reweight</summary>

*Proof.* Write $r=D/2$ for the provisional width. Since $$q=D/128<r/32=D/64,$$ the translated width-$r$ witness claims every coordinate whose parent gap is $<q$.

For a Max witness $x$ as in the statement, let $N_x=\{i:g_i^x(k^f)<q\}$ and $Q_+=\{i:\lambda_i^0=+\}$. Lemma <a href="#lem:up" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:up">11</a> gives $a(N_x)>3/4$. By Lemma <a href="#lem:translation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:translation">6</a>, $N_x\subseteq S_x$; its coordinates are claims of the width-$r$ translation, which qualifies for the unchanged masses $a$. Correctness of that child therefore implies $N_x\subseteq Q_+$. Reweighting the original support gives $$\begin{aligned}
 \bar a(S_x)
   &=\frac85 a(S_x)-\frac45 a(S_x\cap Q_+)\\
   &<\frac85\cdot1-\frac45\cdot\frac34=1.
\end{aligned}$$ The continuation has the same box and operator, so the subsolution inequalities are unchanged. The displayed support bound is all that is needed to keep $x$ a witness.

For Min set $N_y=\{i:g_i^y(k^f)<q\}$ and $Q_-=\{i:\lambda_i^0=-\}$. The same two lemmas show $N_y\subseteq S_y\cap Q_-$ and $b(N_y)>3/4$. Hence $$\bar b(S_y)=\frac85 b(S_y)-\frac45 b(S_y\cap Q_-)
                         <\frac85-\frac45\cdot\frac34=1.$$ The supersolution inequalities still hold in the unchanged box, so $y$ also survives. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-18"></a>
#### Completing the simultaneous induction

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-reweight-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-reweight</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:labelling" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:labelling">5</a>.* Use induction on $R=d+B(\mu)$ from Lemma <a href="#lem:termination" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:termination">9</a>, which has already established termination. We prove that a single returned vector meets every parent obligation.

In the mass base case, $a_i b_i>1$ at every vertex. If $b_i>1$, no Min witness can contain $i$ in its support, and hence no Min witness can claim it; returning $+$ is permitted. If $b_i\le1$, then $a_i>1$, so no Max witness can claim that vertex; returning $-$ is permitted. This proves all simultaneous obligations in Step 1.

In the width base case, comparison with the computed box solution $z$ gives $x\le z\le y$ for every Max witness $x$ and Min witness $y$. At a Max claim, $$z_i\ge A_i+D-P>A_i+D/2,$$ and Step 2 returns $+$. At a Min claim, $z_i\le A_i+P<A_i+D/2$, and it returns $-$. The argument also applies to $D=1$, for which the midpoint is a rational threshold rather than an integer coordinate.

In a nonbase call, all children have smaller rank and satisfy the simultaneous guarantee by induction. The hypotheses of Lemmas <a href="#lem:down" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:down">10</a>–<a href="#lem:reweight" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:reweight">12</a> therefore hold. Fix any Max witness and any index $i$ it claims. Lemma <a href="#lem:down" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:down">10</a> excludes $i$ from $I_-$. If $i\in I_+$, the positive override settles it. Otherwise that witness has a claim outside $I_+$ and survives reweighting by Lemma <a href="#lem:reweight" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:reweight">12</a>. The continuation must label $i$ positively, and no override changes it.

For any Min claim, Lemma <a href="#lem:up" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:up">11</a> excludes $I_+$. A claim in $I_-$ receives the negative override. A claim outside $I_-$ belongs to a surviving Min witness and receives $-$ from the continuation, with no later change. The marks $I_-$ refer to the earlier pivot, but their safety was proved for the fixed parent witnesses and remains valid after the upward pass. The two marked sets may intersect; that intersection contains no claim of either type, so the stipulated negative precedence causes no conflict.

Every vertex receives exactly one label. Since the witnesses and claimed indices in the preceding argument were arbitrary, all their obligations hold together. This completes the induction. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:complexity"></a>
### Counting calls and bit operations

Correctness now gives the complete winning set. To finish the theorem, we must bound the work in terms of binary input length rather than the numerical width of the box. There are two relevant depths: the width can be halved polynomially many times in $L$, but the mass budget can decrease only logarithmically many times in $n$. Because only one child preserves that budget, these depths give a quasipolynomial call count. We then bound the exact arithmetic within each call.

<a id="pm-h-20"></a>
#### The recursion tree

For the initial data of Equation <a href="#eq:main-call" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:main-call">[eq:main-call]</a>, write <a id="eq:initial-resources"></a>$$d_0=\log_2 D_0,\qquad B_0=B(1/n^2),\qquad
 H=d_0+B_0,\qquad s=2048n+3.
 \label{eq:initial-resources}$$ The minimum initial mass product is $1/n^2$. Every path in the call tree has at most $H$ edges by the rank decrease of Lemma <a href="#lem:termination" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:termination">9</a>. At most $B_0$ of its edges can decrease $B$, even if an individual edge decreases it by more than one. At any node there is at most one child preserving $B$, namely the provisional child. There are at most $2(1024n+1)$ loop children and one continuation, so at most $s$ children decrease $B$.

<a id="stmt-lem-node-count"></a>
<div id="lem:node-count" class="lemma">

**Lemma 13** (Number of calls). *The complete main computation makes at most <a id="eq:node-bound"></a>$$\sum_{\ell=0}^{H}\sum_{j=0}^{\min(\ell,B_0)}
       \binom{\ell}{j}s^j
 \ \le\ (H+1)(B_0+1)(1+Hs)^{B_0}
 \label{eq:node-bound}$$ calls, including the initial call.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-node-count"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-node-count</summary>

*Proof.* Consider a node at depth $\ell$ whose path uses $j$ budget-decreasing edges. Their positions can be chosen in $\binom{\ell}{j}$ ways. Order each node’s budget-decreasing children by execution order. At each chosen position there are at most $s$ choices of that ordinal; at each other position there is at most one. This specifies at most $\binom{\ell}{j}s^j$ possible nodes. Sum over all depths $0\le\ell\le H$ and $0\le j\le\min(\ell,B_0)$. For the second inequality, each summand is at most $(Hs)^j\le(1+Hs)^{B_0}$ and there are at most $(H+1)(B_0+1)$ summands. ◻

</details>

This count uses the same recursion principle as the reduced-precision analysis of Parys (Parys 2019, sec. 5) <a id="pm-cite-28"></a>[[Parys2019](#ref-Parys2019)], further developed in (Lehtinen et al. 2022) <a id="pm-cite-29"></a>[[LehtinenParysScheweWojtczak2022](#ref-LehtinenParysScheweWojtczak2022)]: one child retains the bounded parameter while all others improve it. Here the two parameters are the logarithm of an integer width and the budget derived from rational mass products.

<a id="eq:quasipoly-calls"></a>
The explicit encoding bounds both $n$ and $m=|E|$ polynomially in $L+1$, and each original weight has $O(L+1)$ bits. The perturbation and Equation <a href="#eq:initial-width" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:initial-width">[eq:initial-width]</a> imply $$\log_2(W+1)=O(L+\log(n+1)),\qquad
 d_0=O(L+\log(n+1)).$$ Since $\rho=32/25$ is a fixed constant greater than one, $$B_0=1+\lfloor\log_{\rho}(n^2)\rfloor
                          =O(1+\log(n+1)).$$ Thus $H$ and $s$ are polynomial in $L+1$, and $B_0=O(\log(L+2))$. Taking logarithms in the call bound gives $$\begin{split}
 \log_2\bigl(\text{number of calls}\bigr)
 &\le \log_2(H+1)+\log_2(B_0+1)
                         +B_0\log_2(1+Hs)\\
 &=O((\log_2(L+2))^2).
 \end{split}
 \label{eq:quasipoly-calls}$$ This includes $n=1$, for which $B_0=1$.

<a id="pm-h-21"></a>
#### Exact representations

The nested-box property keeps every endpoint and pivot of the main computation inside $[0,D_0]^V$. Each such coordinate and each width therefore uses $O(d_0+1)$ bits. In a basic iteration, an intermediate edge expression is a coordinate plus a transformed weight. It may be negative, but its signed binary length is $O(d_0+\log(W+1)+1)$, polynomial in $L+1$.

Represent a mass by a positive numerator and a positive denominator; reducing fractions is unnecessary. Starting from $1/n$, a mass is multiplied along any path only by numbers in $$\{1,4/3,4/5,8/5\}.$$ Only budget-decreasing edges change the masses, and a path contains at most $B_0$ such edges. Thus each numerator and denominator has $O(B_0+\log(n+1))$ bits. Children receive their own copies of the input masses. In particular, completing one sibling does not multiply the parent masses before another sibling starts. Testing $a_i b_i>1$ uses integer cross-multiplication, and testing $b_i>1$ or applying a constant multiplier also uses numbers of polynomial length. All mass arithmetic is exact.

The proof quantities $B$ and $\mu$, and the peaks, gaps, supports, and witness vectors, need not be stored or computed. The algorithm uses neither a logarithm evaluation for its rank nor an oracle for the existence of a witness. The procedure evaluates $F$ only inside $\ensuremath{\mathsf{Basic}}$, by explicit max/min scans of the edge list.

<a id="pm-h-22"></a>
#### Local cost and uniform implementation

Each call has polynomial nonrecursive work. The mass and width tests are finite scans with the arithmetic just bounded. If the width base case applies, $D<4096$, and the imported iteration bound gives $$1+nD\le1+4095n$$ evaluations, including the final stability test. Every evaluation scans the $m$ explicit edges, adds their transformed weights, takes the relevant extrema, and clips at $n$ endpoints. It acts on integers of polynomial length. The midpoint comparison is implemented as $2z_i>2A_i+D$, which also handles $D=1$.

In a nonbase call, the constants defining the pivot are obtained by exact divisions by powers of two. Each pass has $O(n+1)$ iterations apart from its children’s work. A scan of the $n$ coordinates performs one simultaneous update and tests whether it changed anything. Preparing child inputs and receiving their labels, forming $I_-$ and $I_+$, reweighting, and applying the final overrides likewise use polynomially many operations on polynomial-length integers. Schoolbook arithmetic suffices throughout.

A depth-first implementation runs children sequentially and retains one record per active call. The record holds the parent masses, endpoints, pivot, labels, and current loop position. There are at most $H+1$ active records, each of polynomial size. Stack management is therefore included in the same polynomial local bound. Preprocessing the transformed weights, finding the least power of two in Equation <a href="#eq:initial-width" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:initial-width">[eq:initial-width]</a>, and creating masses $1/n$ take polynomial time. Reading the input and producing the $n$ final labels also do so.

<a id="eq:final-bit-bound"></a>
Multiply this polynomial per-call cost by the call count bounded in Equation <a href="#eq:quasipoly-calls" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:quasipoly-calls">[eq:quasipoly-calls]</a>, and include preprocessing and output. For an absolute constant $C>0$, the complete bound is $$2^{C(\log_2(L+2))^2}.
 \label{eq:final-bit-bound}$$ The constants in the procedure are fixed independently of the game, and every arithmetic and control operation has been specified. This gives a uniform deterministic bit algorithm.

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-node-count-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-node-count</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.* Compute Equation <a href="#eq:main-call" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:main-call">[eq:main-call]</a>, run $\ensuremath{\mathsf{Label}}$, and output the positively labelled vertices. Theorem <a href="#thm:labelling" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:labelling">5</a> guarantees the labels required by every witness. In the initial box, the box solution is a witness of both types, and the argument after Equation <a href="#eq:main-call" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:main-call">[eq:main-call]</a> identifies all its required labels with the bands of Proposition <a href="#prop:game-reduction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:game-reduction">3</a>. The positive band is exactly the zero-threshold winning set, including against every history-dependent opponent. Equation <a href="#eq:final-bit-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:final-bit-bound">[eq:final-bit-bound]</a> bounds the entire computation. Signed binary weights, self-loops, and separately listed parallel edges are all covered by the construction and accounting above. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:full-solution"></a>
### Exact values and optimal positional strategies

We now use the winning-set algorithm to recover the numerical values and strategies of the game. For the input model of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>, write $$\operatorname{val}_w(v)
 =\sup_\sigma\inf_\tau \mathop{\mathrm{MP}}_w(\pi_{v,\sigma,\tau}),$$ where the strategies may depend on the full finite history.

<a id="stmt-cor-full-solution"></a>
<div id="cor:full-solution" class="corollary">

**Corollary 14** (Full game solution). *A uniform deterministic algorithm returns the exact value $\operatorname{val}_w(v)$ as a reduced rational for every $v\in V$ and one positional strategy $\sigma^\star$ for Max and one positional strategy $\tau^\star$ for Min such that, simultaneously for every $v\in V$ and every pair of history-dependent strategies $\sigma,\tau$, $$\mathop{\mathrm{MP}}_w(\pi_{v,\sigma^\star,\tau})\ge\operatorname{val}_w(v),
 \qquad
 \mathop{\mathrm{MP}}_w(\pi_{v,\sigma,\tau^\star})\le\operatorname{val}_w(v).$$ The complete computation takes $2^{O((\log_2(L+2))^2)}$ bit operations.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cor-full-solution"><summary>Proof of stmt-cor-full-solution</summary>

*Proof.* **Positional values.** The uniform form of Ehrenfeucht–Mycielski positional determinacy (Ehrenfeucht and Mycielski 1979) <a id="pm-cite-30"></a>[[EhrenfeuchtMycielski1979](#ref-EhrenfeuchtMycielski1979)], stated explicitly in (Brim et al. 2011, Theorem 1 and the following paragraph) <a id="pm-cite-31"></a>[[BrimEtAl2011](#ref-BrimEtAl2011)], provides one positional strategy for each player attaining the value from all vertices at once, against arbitrary history-dependent opponents. The latter source uses an edge relation without parallel entries. For our convention, replace each explicit edge $e:u\to v$ by $u\to x_e\to v$ through a fresh vertex with a single outgoing edge, with weights $2w(e)$ and $0$. Keep ownership at the original vertices; the owner of $x_e$ is immaterial. The resulting graph has no parallel edges or loops. Expanded histories retain the identity of every original edge. Their averages at even prefixes equal the original averages, and the odd-prefix discrepancy tends to zero. Thus payoffs and the uniform positional guarantees transfer back to our graph, including loops and parallel entries.

Set $W_{\mathrm{in}}=\max_{e\in E}|w(e)|$. Fix both uniform optimal positional strategies. From any vertex the resulting play eventually repeats a simple directed cycle of at most $n$ edges in the original graph, and its mean is the value at that vertex. Consequently every value has a reduced representation $a/b$ with $$1\le b\le n,\qquad |a|\le bW_{\mathrm{in}},
 \qquad -W_{\mathrm{in}}\le a/b\le W_{\mathrm{in}}.$$ The same bounds hold after restricting outgoing choices, provided at least one outgoing edge remains at every vertex.

**Threshold queries and exact reconstruction.** We use the classical reduction from values to threshold queries (Brim et al. 2011, sec. 5.2) <a id="pm-cite-32"></a>[[BrimEtAl2011](#ref-BrimEtAl2011)], keeping track of binary lengths. For integers $p$ and $q>0$, replace each weight by $w_{p/q}(e)=qw(e)-p$. For every infinite play, $$\mathop{\mathrm{MP}}_{w_{p/q}}(\pi)=q\mathop{\mathrm{MP}}_w(\pi)-p.$$ Positional attainment therefore makes the winning set returned by Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> exactly $\{v:\operatorname{val}_w(v)\ge p/q\}$.

Choose $K=1+\lceil\log_2(2(W_{\mathrm{in}}+1)n^2)\rceil$, computable from integer bit lengths. For each vertex, start with the closed interval $[-W_{\mathrm{in}},W_{\mathrm{in}}]$ and make $K$ bisections. Query its midpoint and move the lower endpoint to the midpoint if the vertex belongs to the returned set; otherwise move the upper endpoint. The final interval contains its value and has width $2W_{\mathrm{in}}2^{-K}<1/n^2$. Distinct reduced rationals with positive denominators at most $n$ differ by at least $1/n^2$. To reconstruct the value from an interval $[\ell,h]$, for each $1\le b\le n$ test the integer $a=\lceil b\ell\rceil$ for $a/b\le h$, reduce the retained fractions, and remove repetitions. Since $b(h-\ell)<1$, this tests every possible numerator. The value bound above guarantees exactly one resulting rational. Call this value-vector procedure $\mathsf{Values}(H)$ for any restricted game $H$; the original $n,W_{\mathrm{in}},K$ remain valid for all such calls.

**Separate strategy self-reductions.** An edge joining vertices of the same value need not be optimal. For example, let a Max vertex $u$ have a zero-weight self-loop and a zero-weight edge to a vertex $v$ whose only edge is a self-loop of weight $1$. Both vertices have value $1$, but staying at $u$ yields payoff $0$. We therefore test restrictions of the current game and preserve its entire value vector.

First compute $\mathbf v=\mathsf{Values}(G)$. For Max, start from a fresh copy $H=G$ and process the Max vertices in any fixed order. At a vertex $u$, form a trial $H_e$ for each outgoing edge $e$ by keeping only $e$ at $u$. Keep a trial for which $\mathsf{Values}(H_e)=\mathbf v$, and continue from that trial. Such an edge always exists. Indeed, take the edge chosen at $u$ by a globally optimal positional Max strategy of the current $H$. Restricting Max’s choices cannot increase any value, while that strategy remains available and secures the current entire value vector, so no value decreases either. This proves the invariant $\mathsf{Values}(H)=\mathbf v$.

At the end, Max has one allowed edge at every owned vertex, whereas all of Min’s original choices remain. The resulting single Max strategy therefore secures $\mathbf v$ from every vertex against every Min strategy. To recover Min’s strategy, start again from a fresh copy of the original $G$, not from the Max-restricted game. At each Min vertex keep an outgoing edge whose trial preserves $\mathbf v$. A globally optimal positional Min strategy of the current game supplies such an edge: restricting Min cannot decrease values, and that strategy prevents any increase. The final Min strategy is optimal against every original Max strategy. Thus the two separately recovered strategies have the simultaneous guarantees in the statement.

<a id="eq:threshold-call-bound"></a>
**Call and bit bounds.** Put $m=|E|$. At a vertex, at most its original outdegree many trials are tested, so the two self-reductions together use at most $m$ trial value-vector computations. Including the original computation, there are at most $$Q=nK(1+m)
 \label{eq:threshold-call-bound}$$ threshold calls. Every midpoint has denominator dividing $2^K$ and a numerator of $O(\log(W_{\mathrm{in}}+1)+K)$ bits. Each transformed weight has the same order of bit length. Since $K=O(L+\log(n+1))$, the full encoding length of every queried game is $O(L+mK)$, polynomial in $L+1$. Reconstruction, reduced-rational comparison, and copying restricted edge lists also have polynomial bit cost. The explicit encoding bounds $n,m$ polynomially in $L+1$, so $Q$ is polynomial. Applying Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> to these polynomial-length games and multiplying by $Q$ gives $2^{O((\log_2(L+2))^2)}$ bit operations, including the polynomial-size value and strategy output. ◻

</details>

The preceding reduction also applies to the companion algorithm. Here the additional issue is that threshold queries depend on earlier answers, so the winning-set error must be controlled at each query.

<a id="stmt-cor-randomized-full-solution"></a>
<div id="cor:randomized-full-solution" class="corollary">

**Corollary 15** (Randomized full game solution). *For the game model of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>, a uniform randomized algorithm returns all exact values and both globally optimal positional strategies specified in Corollary <a href="#cor:full-solution" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:full-solution">14</a> with probability at least $7/8$ for the entire output. Its running time is $2^{O((\log_2(L+2))^2)}$ bit operations on every random tape.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cor-randomized-full-solution"><summary>Proof of stmt-cor-randomized-full-solution</summary>

*Proof.* The companion’s Theorem 1.1 (OpenAI 2026) <a id="pm-cite-33"></a>[[OpenAI2026Randomized](#ref-OpenAI2026Randomized)] returns the whole winning set correctly with probability at least $7/8$ and has the quasipolynomial bit bound on every tape. Use the reduction above, whose number of threshold queries is bounded by the polynomial $Q$ in Equation <a href="#eq:threshold-call-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:threshold-call-bound">[eq:threshold-call-bound]</a>. For each query, take $r=2\lceil\log_2(8Q)\rceil+1$ independent fresh runs and use the coordinatewise majority of their winning sets. Conditional on the previous history, the current query is fixed. The amplified set is correct whenever more than half of the runs return the whole correct set, and its conditional failure probability is at most $$\sum_{j=\lceil r/2\rceil}^{r}\binom rj(1/8)^j
 \le 2^r(1/8)^{r/2}=2^{-r/2}<\frac1{8Q}.$$ A first-error union bound over the adaptive queries makes every threshold answer correct with probability at least $7/8$. On this event the preceding deterministic argument gives all exact values and both globally optimal strategies. On other tapes, if reconstruction does not yield a unique rational or no value-preserving trial is found, the reduction stops with zero at every value coordinate and the first listed outgoing edge at every owned vertex. All loops have the stated caps even then. Since $r=O(\log(L+2))$ and all queried encodings retain their polynomial bound on every tape, the total bit bound remains quasipolynomial on every tape, including random-bit generation. This transfer uses only the winning-set interface. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:energy-credits"></a>
### Fixed- and minimum-credit energy games

<a id="eq:energy-objective"></a>
The preceding algorithms also compute the initial credits needed in one-resource energy games. Retain the game model of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>. For a start $v\in V$ and an integer credit $c\ge0$, call $(v,c)$ *energy-winning* if Max has a strategy such that, against every history-dependent Min strategy, the induced play satisfies $$c+\sum_{t=0}^{j-1}w(e_t)\ge0\qquad\text{for every }j\ge0.
 \label{eq:energy-objective}$$ There is one accumulated resource and no upper bound or saturation. Write $c_{\min}(v)$ for the least nonnegative integer credit for which $(v,c)$ is energy-winning, and put $c_{\min}(v)=\infty$ if there is none. A positional Max policy specifies one labelled outgoing edge at each Max vertex.

The reductions connecting these problems to mean-payoff games are classical. Bouyer, Fahrenberg, Larsen, Markey, and Srba formulate the fixed-credit lower-bound problem and reduce it to mean payoff using adversary-controlled returns to the start (Bouyer et al. 2008, sec. 2.1 and Proposition 12) <a id="pm-cite-34"></a>[[BouyerEtAl2008](#ref-BouyerEtAl2008)]. Our construction places the initial credit on each return edge. Brim, Chaloupka, Doyen, Gentilini, and Raskin distinguish the unknown-credit and minimum-credit problems, record the unknown-credit/mean-payoff equivalence, and characterize minimum credits by an energy progress measure (Brim et al. 2011, sec. 2 and Theorems 3 and 5) <a id="pm-cite-35"></a>[[BrimEtAl2011](#ref-BrimEtAl2011)]. The reset construction below lets us apply Corollary <a href="#cor:full-solution" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:full-solution">14</a> to a prescribed-credit instance, recovering a policy when that instance is winning. A bound on finite minimum credits then permits exact binary search.

<a id="stmt-cor-energy-credits"></a>
<div id="cor:energy-credits" class="corollary">

**Corollary 16** (Fixed and minimum energy credits). *Uniform deterministic algorithms have the following guarantees.*

1.  *Given $v$ and a nonnegative binary integer $c$, decide whether $(v,c)$ is energy-winning. On a positive answer, return a positional Max policy satisfying Equation <a href="#eq:energy-objective" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:energy-objective">[eq:energy-objective]</a> from that start and credit against every history-dependent Min strategy.*

2.  *Given $v$, return the exact $c_{\min}(v)$, including the possible answer $\infty$. On a finite answer, return a positional Max policy winning from $v$ at credit $c_{\min}(v)$.*

*The fixed-credit computation takes $2^{O((\log_2(L_c+2))^2)}$ bit operations, where $L_c$ is the full explicit input length including the start and credit. The minimum-credit computation takes $2^{O((\log_2(L+2))^2)}$ bit operations for the original game length $L$. Handling every vertex by separate per-start searches, including a separate policy for every finite answer, has the same form of total bit bound.*

*Each policy may depend on the queried start and credit; it also wins from that same start at every larger credit.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cor-energy-credits"><summary>Proof of stmt-cor-energy-credits</summary>

<a id="eq:energy-reset-equivalence"></a>
*Proof.* **Fixed credit by resetting.** For the fixed pair $(v,c)$ construct a mean-payoff game $R_{v,c}$. Replace every $u\in V$ by an entry $u_{\mathrm{in}}$ owned by Min and an exit $u_{\mathrm{out}}$ owned by the original owner of $u$. Add the two edges $$u_{\mathrm{in}}\xrightarrow{c}v_{\mathrm{in}},
 \qquad
 u_{\mathrm{in}}\xrightarrow{0}u_{\mathrm{out}},$$ and replace each labelled original edge $e:u\to z$ by its own edge $$u_{\mathrm{out}}\xrightarrow{w(e)}z_{\mathrm{in}}.$$ Start at $v_{\mathrm{in}}$. The game is total and has $2n$ vertices and $m+2n$ edges, where $m=|E|$. The reset at $u=v$ is a nonnegative self-loop, including when $c=0$, and distinct original parallel edges remain distinct. If $U(R_{v,c})$ denotes its zero-threshold winning set, then $$(v,c)\text{ is energy-winning in }G
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad v_{\mathrm{in}}\in U(R_{v,c}).
 \label{eq:energy-reset-equivalence}$$

For the forward implication, use an energy-winning strategy from $(v,c)$ and restart its history after each reset. In any transformed prefix, every completed segment has original-edge sum at least $-c$ and then receives the reset weight $+c$. Its total is therefore nonnegative. The unfinished segment has sum at least $-c$; the intervening zero edges do not change this bound. Every transformed prefix sum is consequently at least $-c$. Dividing by its length shows that the lower limiting average is nonnegative. This argument also covers no resets and infinitely many resets, and does not assume that the original energy strategy is positional.

Conversely, if $v_{\mathrm{in}}\in U(R_{v,c})$, Corollary <a href="#cor:full-solution" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:full-solution">14</a> returns a positional Max strategy for $R_{v,c}$ guaranteeing nonnegative mean payoff from that start. Restrict its choices at Max exits to obtain a positional policy in $G$. Suppose a consistent original prefix of length $j$ from $v$ had weight $q<-c$. In the reset game Min can choose continuation at its entries, follow that prefix at its owned exits, and then reset. This is a closed walk from $v_{\mathrm{in}}$ with weight $q+c<0$ and length $2j+1$. Positionality of the Max strategy makes the same walk consistent on every repetition. Min could therefore repeat it forever, giving mean payoff $(q+c)/(2j+1)<0$, a contradiction. Thus all original prefixes have weight at least $-c$. This proves the equivalence and the positive-answer policy guarantee; only the Max strategy from the full-solution call is used.

**A finite credit bound and exact search.** For the original weights put <a id="eq:energy-credit-bound"></a>$$W_{\mathrm{in}}=\max_{e\in E}|w(e)|,
 \qquad H=(n-1)W_{\mathrm{in}}.
 \label{eq:energy-credit-bound}$$ In particular $W_{\mathrm{in}}$ and $H$ may be zero. An energy winner with any finite credit is an original zero-threshold mean-payoff winner, since its prefix sums are bounded below. Conversely, the cycle-deletion estimate in the proof of Proposition <a href="#prop:game-reduction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:game-reduction">3</a> gives a positional Max policy on the original winning region for which every consistent prefix has weight at least $-H$. Indeed, its permitted simple cycles have nonnegative original sum, and deleting such cycles from a prefix leaves at most $n-1$ edges, each of weight at least $-W_{\mathrm{in}}$. Consequently, for the original winning set $U(G)$, <a id="eq:energy-finite-equivalence"></a>$$c_{\min}(v)<\infty\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad v\in U(G),
 \qquad
 v\in U(G)\quad\Longrightarrow\quad 0\le c_{\min}(v)\le H.
 \label{eq:energy-finite-equivalence}$$ The algorithm uses this existence bound as a known winning endpoint; it need not compute the potential used in that proposition.

Compute $U(G)$ once by Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> and output $\infty$ for starts outside it. For a winning start, the fixed-credit predicate is monotone: a strategy that wins at $c$ still wins at every larger credit. Binary search with a formal losing lower endpoint $\ell=-1$ and the known winning upper endpoint $h=H$. While $h-\ell>1$, query $\lfloor(\ell+h)/2\rfloor$ by Equation <a href="#eq:energy-reset-equivalence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:energy-reset-equivalence">[eq:energy-reset-equivalence]</a> and replace the endpoint having the same answer. The sentinel itself is never queried. This takes at most $\lceil\log_2(H+1)\rceil$ fixed-credit decisions and returns the exact least winning integer. When $H=0$ the initial gap is one, so no search query is made and the answer is zero. This includes both $n=1$ and all-zero weights; any start without a finite credit has already been excluded by $U(G)$. To return a policy at the minimum, apply Corollary <a href="#cor:full-solution" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:full-solution">14</a> to $R_{v,c_{\min}(v)}$ and restrict its Max choices as above.

**Bit complexity and output size.** Let $b_c$ be the binary length of the supplied nonnegative credit, so $b_c=O(1+\log_2(c+1))$. The explicit reset game has encoding length $$O\bigl(L+n(b_c+\log_2(n+1))\bigr),$$ which is polynomial in $L_c$. The credit is copied into only $n$ edge records; no array indexed by its numerical value is constructed. Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> decides membership in its winning set, and a single application of Corollary <a href="#cor:full-solution" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:full-solution">14</a> on a positive answer supplies the policy, within the fixed-credit bound claimed.

For minimum credits, $H$ and every queried credit have $O(1+\log_2(n+1)+\log_2(W_{\mathrm{in}}+1))=O(L+1)$ bits. All queried encodings therefore have polynomial length in $L+1$. For all vertices, the outer reduction makes at most $1+n\lceil\log_2(H+1)\rceil$ direct winning-set calls, including the original one, and at most one full-solution call for each requested finite-credit policy. These counts are polynomial in $L+1$. Polynomially many calls on polynomial-length inputs preserve $2^{O((\log_2(L+2))^2)}$ bit complexity. Computing $H$, the binary search arithmetic, and copying the policies have polynomial bit cost. The output also has polynomial length: each finite credit has the stated bit bound, and each per-start policy lists one original edge at each Max vertex. No energy-indexed array is used in the search. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:tropical"></a>
### Tropical feasibility and fixed corank

Akian, Gaubert, and Guterman identify the coordinates that can be finite in a solution of tropical inequalities with the winning states of an associated mean-payoff game (Akian, Gaubert, and Guterman 2012, Theorem 3.2 and Corollary 3.7) <a id="pm-cite-36"></a>[[AkianGaubertGuterman2012](#ref-AkianGaubertGuterman2012)]. Together with Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>, this gives binary-length bounds for tropical feasibility and for rank within a fixed distance of the number of columns. We use the conventions $a+(-\infty)=-\infty$ and $\max\varnothing=-\infty$.

<a id="stmt-cor-tropical-computation"></a>
<div id="cor:tropical-computation" class="corollary">

**Corollary 17** (Tropical feasibility and fixed corank). *For explicitly listed $m\times n$ matrices $A,B$ and $m$-dimensional vectors $c,d$ over $\mathbb Q\cup\{-\infty\}$, feasibility of $$\max\bigl\{c_i,\max_j(A_{ij}+x_j)\bigr\}
 \leq \max\bigl\{d_i,\max_j(B_{ij}+x_j)\bigr\}
 \qquad\text{for every row }i$$ is decidable deterministically in $2^{O((\log(L+2))^2)}$ bit operations, where $L$ is the complete explicit binary input length. Variables range over $\mathbb R\cup\{-\infty\}$; feasibility with all coordinates finite is decidable within the same bound. Equalities between the two displayed maxima are allowed, interpreted as pairs of inequalities. For a homogeneous system ($c=d=-\infty$), one can also compute the maximal finite-coordinate support of a solution, including the empty support. In particular, the same bound decides feasibility of max-atom systems $z\leq\max(x,y)+a$, with rational constants $a$ and finite real, equivalently rational, variables.*

*For each fixed integer $k\geq0$, a deterministic algorithm with bit bound $2^{O_k((\log(L+2))^2)}$ decides whether an explicitly listed $m\times n$ matrix over $\mathbb Q\cup\{-\infty\}$, with $m\geq n\geq1$, has tropical rank at least $n-k$. Here tropical rank is the largest order of a square submatrix whose permutation sums have a finite, unique maximum. The rank is zero if no such nonempty submatrix exists.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cor-tropical-computation"><summary>Proof of stmt-cor-tropical-computation</summary>

*Proof.* **Integer data and homogeneous supports.** Let $Q$ be the product of the positive denominators of the finite input coefficients, taking $Q=1$ when there are none. Multiplying every finite coefficient and variable by $Q$ preserves the inequalities and the sets of finite coordinates. It also preserves the comparisons between permutation sums that define tropical rank. The bit length of $Q$ is at most one plus the sum of the denominator bit lengths, so the scaled integer data have polynomial binary length.

Consider first a homogeneous system <a id="eq:tropical-homogeneous"></a>$$\max_j(C_{ij}+z_j)\leq\max_j(D_{ij}+z_j)
 \qquad\text{for every }i.
 \label{eq:tropical-homogeneous}$$ The support of $z$ is $\{j:z_j\ne-\infty\}$. To obtain a total game, apply the preprocessing of (Akian, Gaubert, and Guterman 2012, sec. 2.1) <a id="pm-cite-37"></a>[[AkianGaubertGuterman2012](#ref-AkianGaubertGuterman2012)]. Discard a row whose left side is identically $-\infty$. If a right side is identically $-\infty$, every variable with a finite coefficient on that row’s left side must equal $-\infty$. Record these coordinates, substitute $-\infty$ for them throughout the system, and repeat. Each step removes a row or a variable, so this takes polynomial time. If no variables remain, the maximal support is empty. Otherwise add the tautologies $z_j\leq z_j$ for the remaining variables.

The resulting bipartite game has a Min vertex for each remaining column and a Max vertex for each row. A finite $C_{ij}$ gives an edge from column $j$ to row $i$ of weight $-C_{ij}$; a finite $D_{ij}$ gives an edge from row $i$ to column $j$ of weight $D_{ij}$. Every vertex has an outgoing edge. Theorem 3.2 of (Akian, Gaubert, and Guterman 2012) <a id="pm-cite-38"></a>[[AkianGaubertGuterman2012](#ref-AkianGaubertGuterman2012)] identifies its winning column vertices with the maximal support of a solution. The recorded deleted coordinates belong to no support. The union of all supports is attained, because coordinatewise maxima of finitely many solutions are again solutions; the identically $-\infty$ solution realizes the empty case.

The cited game counts a column–row–column pair as one turn. Counting the two edges separately halves the mean payoff; the contribution of an unmatched edge tends to zero. The source uses upper limiting averages; positional optimality makes their nonnegative winning set coincide with that for our lower limiting averages. The game has polynomially many vertices and edges, with weights copied or negated from the scaled coefficients or equal to zero. Its full binary encoding is polynomial in $L+1$, so Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> computes the maximal support within the asserted bound.

**Affine and finite feasibility.** After scaling, append $c$ to $A$ and $d$ to $B$ as a new column, and introduce one coordinate $t$. The homogeneous system is $$\max\bigl\{c_i+t,\max_j(A_{ij}+z_j)\bigr\}
 \leq\max\bigl\{d_i+t,\max_j(B_{ij}+z_j)\bigr\}.$$ Every affine solution $x$ gives a homogeneous solution $(x,0)$. Conversely, a homogeneous solution with finite $t$ gives the affine solution $x_j=z_j-t$. Thus affine feasibility asks whether the new coordinate belongs to the maximal support. Feasibility with all original coordinates finite asks whether that support contains all $n+1$ coordinates. A coordinate eliminated during preprocessing is absent from the computed support, so any test requiring it fails; this includes $t$ if it is forced to $-\infty$. This is the homogenization underlying (Akian, Gaubert, and Guterman 2012, Theorem 3.5 and Corollaries 3.4 and 3.7) <a id="pm-cite-39"></a>[[AkianGaubertGuterman2012](#ref-AkianGaubertGuterman2012)]. Equalities are handled by imposing both inequalities.

A max-atom $z\leq\max(x,y)+a$ is a homogeneous row with one term on the left and two on the right. Apply the all-finite test. With integer coefficients, taking the floor of each finite coordinate preserves every inequality: flooring commutes with integer translation and with finite maxima. Scaling back therefore gives a rational solution whenever a real one exists. This also explains the integral-solution interface in (Akian, Gaubert, and Guterman 2012, Proposition 3.9, Corollary 3.10 and Remark 3.15) <a id="pm-cite-40"></a>[[AkianGaubertGuterman2012](#ref-AkianGaubertGuterman2012)].

**Fixed corank.** If $n\leq k$, the rank threshold is nonpositive and the answer is yes. Otherwise set $r=n-k$. The rank criterion of (Akian, Gaubert, and Guterman 2012, Corollaries 4.16–4.17) <a id="pm-cite-41"></a>[[AkianGaubertGuterman2012](#ref-AkianGaubertGuterman2012)] says that the rank is at least $r$ exactly when some set of $r$ columns is tropically independent. For each such set let $M$ be the resulting $m\times r$ matrix. Its columns are dependent precisely when there is a vector $z\not\equiv-\infty$ such that every row maximum is attained at least twice or equals $-\infty$. Equivalently, $z$ satisfies the homogeneous system <a id="eq:tropical-dependence"></a>$$M_{ij}+z_j\leq\max_{\ell\ne j}(M_{i\ell}+z_\ell)
 \qquad(1\leq i\leq m,\ 1\leq j\leq r).
 \label{eq:tropical-dependence}$$ Its maximal support is therefore empty exactly when the selected columns are independent. This is the inequality form of the game reduction in (Akian, Gaubert, and Guterman 2012, Theorem 4.9) <a id="pm-cite-42"></a>[[AkianGaubertGuterman2012](#ref-AkianGaubertGuterman2012)]. The row index $(i,j)$ retains the column excluded from Max’s next choice. Empty right sides and all-$-\infty$ columns are covered by the support computation above, including when $r=1$.

There are at most $mr$ inequalities, with $r$ coefficients on each side, so each test has polynomial encoding length. There are $\binom nr=\binom nk\leq n^k$ tests. For fixed $k$, their total bit cost is $2^{O_k((\log(L+2))^2)}$, as claimed. ◻

</details>

Strongly polynomial algorithms have been claimed using max-atoms and tropical optimization (Truffet 2025, 2026) <a id="pm-cite-43"></a>[[Truffet2025MaxAtoms](#ref-Truffet2025MaxAtoms), [Truffet2026SubstitutionV4](#ref-Truffet2026SubstitutionV4)]. Appendix A of the randomized companion gives a two-variable counterexample to the printed variable-selection rule in `arXiv:2603.26423v4` (OpenAI 2026) <a id="pm-cite-44"></a>[[OpenAI2026Randomized](#ref-OpenAI2026Randomized)]. That comparison is specific to the version and rule examined; it neither assesses the distinct 2025 max-atom proof nor transfers to a revised procedure. It has no role in the proofs of this article.
